3.4.1 Implementation of Eklavya Model Residential School scheme

Scheme on Eklavya Model Residential School (EMRS) was launched in
2002-03 in Rajasthan for imparting quality education to the tribal students
from standard VI to XII. As per the scheme, these schools were residential
schools and would provide free education and basic amenities like food,
uniform, sweater, shoes, socks, etc. to the students. Under the scheme, funds

were to be provided as 100 per cent grant from Government of India (Gol).
The tribal population of Rajasthan, as per 2011 Census, is 92.39 lakh which is



Chapter Ill Compliance Audit

13.48 per cent of the total population (685.48 lakh) of Rajasthan. As on date,
eight EMRSs are being operated under this scheme. In addition, six”
additional EMRSs are under construction.

The Ministry of Tribal Affairs (MTA), Gol, issues guidelines, makes
modifications, reviews and monitors the implementation of the scheme. At the
state level, the Principal Secretary, Tribal Area Development Department
(TAD) is in overall charge of the scheme. Commissioner, TAD, Udaipur, is
responsible for overall monitoring and supervising the scheme. At the district
level, Project Officer Tribal (POT) or Chief Executive Officer (CEO), Zila
Parishad (ZP), is responsible for implementation of the scheme.

Audit ssed the implementation of the scheme in the State of Rajasthan, by
reviewing the functioning of four EMRSs”° (two girls schools and two boys
schools) which were operational and also evaluating the progress of all six
schools which were under construction. Three EMRSs (Hanotiya, Kotra and
Newai), with classes from standard VI to XII with a capacity of 350 students
each, were started from the academic session 2002-03, while one EMRS
(Timerva) having classes from standard VI to VIII with a capacity of 210
students was started from the academic session 2012-13.

actual enrolment their-against during 2011-14 was as under:

Responding to the audit observation, the State Government stated (October
2014) that awareness programmes to enhance the enrolment were being
organised.

3.4.1.2 Shortage of teaching staff

As per paragraph IV (viii) of the guidelines, appointment of teachers would be
handled entirely by the society chosen by the State Government and in the
manner deemed most suitable.

Audit scrutiny revealed that there was no mechanism for recruiting regular
teaching staff for schools under EMRS scheme. All the teachers appointed in
test checked EMRSs were on deputation from education department or
appointed as casual staff, as evident from the position of sanctioned and
working strength of teaching staff shown below:

1 Hanotiya (Baran) | 18 | 6 7 B 5 (28)
2__| Kotra (Udaipur) 26 3 3 [6 | 2007
3__| Newai (Tonk 25 18 [| - [18 728
4 _[Timerva(Pratapgarh) | 6 | 4 [4 2 83)

Total 75 31 [0 [था 34 (45)

Source: Information provided by the department.

In four test checked EMRSs, 41 teaching staff were working on
deputation/contractual basis against the sanctioned post of 75 as on 31March
2014. Thus, there was a shortage of 34 (45 per cent) teachers. The maximum
shortage of teachers (77 per cent) was in EMRS, Kotra (Udaipur), which is
one of the major reasons for the low percentage of enrolment and maximum
drop outs in the EMRS.

There were 31 teachers on deputation from the very beginning and remaining
10 teachers were appointed as vidyarthi mitra on contractual basis, though
declared illegal and unconstitutional as per the decision (April 2014) of
Honourable High Court. Three of the contractual teachers appointed in EMRS
Hanotiya (Baran), did not have the requisite teaching qualification”. Thus,
there was no mechanism of recruiting staff on regular basis teachers.

The State Government stated (October 2014) that EMRS Kotra is in remote
tural area, therefore, teachers do not apply for deputation. However, walk-in
interviews were being held for selection of teachers on deputation to overcome
the shortage and to replace vidyarthi mitra in compliance to the decision
(April 2014) of Hon’ble High Court.

Fact remains that the shortage of teachers defeated the very purpose of the

scheme i.e. improving the quality of education being imparted to tribal
children. It also affected the pass percentage and enrolment in the EMRSs.

3.4.1.3 Low pass percentage in Board Examinations

As per paragraph IV(IX) of the guidelines, the State Government was required
to maintain the highest quality in the selection of teachers and staff for
academic and extracurricular excellence.

The results of X standard Board examination during the period 2011-14 are
tabulated below:

Hanotiya (Boys) 45 21 (49) 48 9 69) 38 13 (34)
Kotra (Boys) 40 4 (10) a 13 (28) 34 4 (12)

Source: Information provided by EMRSs

Pass percentage of EMRS, Newai, Tonk (upto 86 per cent) was satisfactory.
However, the Pass percentages were quite low in the other EMRSs and ranged
between 10 and 49 per cent. The pass percentage was very low in case of
Kotra.

The State Government stated (October 2014) that these EMRSs were located
in remote areas and efforts were being made for better results.

Some of the factors leading to low pass percentage could be attributed to the
disinclination of teachers to be posted in remote areas, contractual teachers
with poor qualification, etc.

3.4.1.4 Dropout rate

The drop out percentage in four test checked EMRSs, ranged between 1.7
(EMRS Newai) to 7.2 (EMRS Kotra) per cent in three EMRSs while in
EMRS, Timerva (Pratapgarh) there was no drop out during 2011-12 to
2013-14.

The range of dropout in girls EMRS Newai was 1.7 to 4.4 per cent and nil in
EMRS Timerva, while the drop out of boys in EMRSs Hanotiya and Kotra
ranged between 2.9 and 7.2 per cent during 2011-12 to 2013-14.

State Government stated (October 2014) that efforts were being made to
improve the quality of education.

3.4.1.5 Lack of Infrastructure facilities

EMRS should provide items like ramp, rain water harvesting system, science
laboratory, compound wall, assured reliable water supply, toilets, housing for
warden, security and staff etc.

89



Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

(a) Operating science classes without science laboratories

. The State Government announced in the budget session of 2010-11 for
starting science faculty in EMRS from academic session 2011-12. During
audit, it was seen that in EMRS Newai (Tonk), the classes under science
streams i.e. Physics, Chemistry and Biology for classes XI and XII from
academic year 2012-13 and 2013-14, were being taught without any science
laboratory.

On being pointed out, the State Government stated (October 2014) that
construction work of Physics, Chemistry and Biology laboratories had started.
This implies that batches of students had passed 12" standard during 2012-13
and 2013-14 without having benefit of science laboratories.

In EMRS Hanotiya (Baran) and Kotra (Udaipur), no science teacher had been
recruited and hence no student opted for the science stream. On being pointed
out, Principals of EMRS Hanotiya (Baran) and Kotra (Udaipur) while
admitting the facts, stated (June 2014) that in absence of a science teacher,
students did not apply for admission and the science stream could not be
started.

. The State Government sanctioned (February 2011) ₹ै 6.00 lakh to
EMRS Kotra (Udaipur) for opening Physics, Chemistry and Biology
laboratories from academic session 2011-12 and this was transferred (March
2011) to POT Udaipur for purchase of items for these laboratories. Audit
scrutiny revealed that the material purchased for हैं 4.49 lakh for science
laboratory, issued to EMRS Kotra (Udaipur) in August 2011, remained
unutilised for three sessions (2011-12 to 2013-14), due to non-opening of
science faculty in the absence of a science teacher. No student had taken
admission in the science stream. Subsequently, POT, Udaipur issued orders
(December 2013) for transfer of these materials to Model Public Residential
School (MPRS), Dhikali (Udaipur) and the same were transferred in April
2014. However, Headmaster, MPRS, Dhikali (Udaipur), intimated (June 2014)
that these items were neither demanded nor used by them.

State Government admitting the facts (October 2014) stated that for better
utilisation, the material was transferred to MPRS Dhikali (Udaipur), where
science stream was operational.

(b) Lack of computer aided learning

As per the Annexure to EMRS scheme guidelines, computer laboratories
should have been included in the lay out plan of the EMRSs. During Test
check of EMRS Timerva (Pratapgarh) and EMRS Hanotyia (Baran), it was
observed that EMRS Timerva (Pratapgarh) neither had any computer nor
computer laboratory, while EMRS Hanotiya (Baran) had 10 computers but
computer teacher was not appointed resulting in non-utilisation of these
equipment and depriving the students from computer-aided learning (CAL).
The State Government stated (October 2014) that principal of all EMRSs had
been asked to send proposals for required equipment. Computers would be

facility was available, internet connectivity would also be provided.

guidelines. State Government stated (October 2014) that concerned District

rainwater harvesting structures. However, it was noticed that in school
buildings of EMRS, Newai (Tonk), Kotra (Udaipur) and Hanotiya (Baran), the
rainwater harvesting system did not exist. This resulted in non-conservation of
rain water. The State Government stated (October 2014) that construction of
rainwater harvesting structure was not included in the concerned work plan.

(ix) | In EMRS Kotra (Udaipur), the water was supplied from the tube well
only when electricity was available. However, due to damage and leakages in
pipeline and water tank, water could not be stored when it was being supplied.
This led to water scarcity in the school/hostel. The State Government stated
(October 2014) that the work of construction of over head tank had been
sanctioned (February 2014). However, the construction work has still not been
started (October 2014).

(x) During inspection, audit observed that bathrooms and toilets of the
school and hostel of EMRS Kotra (Udaipur) were lying chocked since 2004.
However, State Government informed (October 2014) that the same have now
been got repaired.

(व) Health check-up

TAD directed (August 2011) conduction of regular medical check-up of
students studying in residential schools. In all test checked EMRSs height and
weight of students were regularly monitored. It was seen that in EMRS
Timerva (Pratapgarh), 10 out of 60 students in 2012-13 and 15 out of 90
students in 2013-14 were found underweight by the nurse. However, no doctor
visited the EMRS for medical check-up or for prescribing treatment to
underweight students as intimated (June 2014) by the EMRS. This defeated
the purpose of instructions of TAD regarding health check-up.

State Government admitting the facts (October 2014), ensured that health
register for each student would be maintained for recording regular
information of health check up.

3.4.1.6 Construction work of buildings for new EMRS

As per Rule 298 of Public Works Financial & Accounts Rules (PWF & ARs)
‘availability of the site is a pre-requisite for planning and designing of a work
and as such no department of the Government should call upon the working
agency to initiate planning until the site is physically in its possession’.
Further, Gol also enjoined (December 2009) upon the State Government that
while sending their proposals for EMRS, the department should specifically
ensure that the available land is free from all encumbrances.

Ministry of Tribal Affairs, Gol sanctioned (September 2010) हैं 72 crore
(@ ₹ 12 crore per EMRS) for establishing EMRS in six villages” of
Rajasthan and released हैं 66 crore between September 2010 and February
2013. The work of construction of six EMRS buildings was awarded (October
2010) to RSRDC, Jaipur. TAD released funds of € 51.42 crore to RSRDC. Of
this, RSRDC incurred an expenditure of ¥ 49.56 crore. It was seen that UCs of
₹ 43.02 crore only were sent to the TAD department as detailed below:

Audit scrutiny revealed (June 2014) that construction work of all six EMRS
could not be completed (June 2014) due to delayed land allotment, delayed
handing over of land due to encroachment and non-transfer of remaining funds
(₹ 14.58 crore). Thus, failure of TAD to provide land, free from all
encumbrances and sufficient funds to RSRDC, resulted in non-completion of
EMRS buildings even after lapse of about two years from the stipulated date
of completion (June to September 2012). The State Government intimated
(October 2014) that all works are under progress.

As per paragraph No. V (ii) of the guidelines, states are encouraged to use fuel
saving or renewable energy technologies in the school buildings, but no such
technology was used in EMRSs buildings. Department accepted (October
2014) the facts.

3.4.1.7 Less EMRS for girls in comparison to boys

As per paragraph III (c) of EMRS guidelines, the number of seats for boys and
girls should be equal. Scrutiny revealed that out of eight running EMRS with
2660 seats, six’® EMRS with 2100 seats were for boys, while only two”
EMRS (25 per cent) with 560 seats (21 per cent) were for girls. This was in
contravention of the scheme guidelines. Thus, equal opportunities in education
for boys and girls were not provided.

On being pointed out (June-July 2014) the State Government stated (October
2014) that equal number of girls and boys would be admitted in the six new
EMRSs under construction.

3.4.1.8 Financial Position

The grants (Capital and Revenue) received from Gol by the State Government
were to be transferred to the Project Implementing Agencies, ie
Commissioner, Tribal Area Development Department (TAD), Udaipur, within
30 days. These funds were further transferred to concerned POTs or CEOs
(ZPs), for incurring expenditure on the EMRS under their jurisdiction.

3.4.1.9 Monitoring of the Scheme
(a) Constitution of society

The scheme on EMRS envisaged the setting up of a society at State level to
handle the school admissions, appointment of staff, personal matters and
overall management of schools, accept donations/contributions and grants
from individuals and Institutions. A Management Committee for each EMRS
was also required to be set up to monitor day to day activities and management
of the EMRSs.

It was noticed that no State-level Society had been constituted. Management
Committees were also not constituted in three™’ out of four test checked
EMRSs. State Government in reply stated (October 2014) that a State-level
society namely ‘Rajasthan Residential Educational Institutional Society’ has
been constituted, and the proposal for running of the EMRSs through the
Society is under consideration of the Government. The fact remains that
society was not functioning (October 2014).

(b) Inspection of schools

Commissioner, TAD, instructed (April 2007) all CEOs, ZP, (Rural
Development Cell) and POT, to inspect residential schools in their jurisdiction
every week and submit their reports to Deputy Director (Education) TAD,
Udaipur.

In EMRSs Timerva (Pratapgarh) and Hanotiya (Baran) although inspection
was carried out by the authorities but records of inspection and corrective
action taken after inspection were not maintained. Only 6 and 11 inspections
were carried out in EMRS Newai (Tonk) and Kotra (Udaipur) respectively
during 2011-12 to 2013-14, but action taken on the findings of these
inspections were not intimated.

The State Government stated (October 2014) that inspections were being
carried out regularly by concerned officers. The reply was not acceptable as no
records relating to these inspections were available. Thus, lack/ insufficiency
of inspections and non-maintenance of record reflect monitoring lapses.
Online monitoring mechanism also was not developed, as required under the
guidelines.

3.4.1.10 Conclusions

Eklavya Model Residential Schools were started with the objectives of
imparting quality education to tribal students. It was seen that the State-level
society and management committees at school level that were required to be
constituted to manage the affairs of EMRSs, were not functioning. The
enrolment of students in schools was less than their capacity and retention of
students was also low. There was a shortage of teachers and the science stream

*°  Kotra (Udaipur), Newai (Tonk) and Timerva (Pratapgarh).

96



Chapter III Compliance Audit

was functioning without laboratories. The hostels had insufficient facilities
which were impacting the overall performance of the scheme.

The progress in construction of new EMRSs was slow and even after a lapse
of more than two years from the stipulated date of completion, these were
incomplete. This deprived the students from benefits of the scheme.

3.4.2. Implementation of schemes for Prevention of discrimination
against Girls

Girls are the most vulnerable members of Indian society. The life of a girl
reflects elements of discrimination at every step of her life. In the social milieu
of Rajasthan, gender discrimination against girls and women occurs at every
stage of their life. The girl is discriminated at every step - at home, in school,
community and society at large. Socio-economic background, disability and
location of residence (rural-urban) further compound her vulnerabilities and
work to her disadvantage.

Deep-rooted preference for sons and aversion to daughters, aided by
technological advances, led to a steep decline in child sex ratio in India and
particularly in Rajasthan and this presents an alarming scenario. Census of
2011 highlighted an increasingly adverse child (0-6 years) sex ratio in
Rajasthan showing a 21 point decline from 909 girls (2001) to 888 per 1,000
boys (2011). This decline was also reflected in the overall sex ratio in
Rajasthan which was 928:1,000 as compared to the All India sex ratio of
943:1,000. Female literacy rate in Rajasthan was also 52.1 per cent as per
2011 Census as against the overall literacy rate of 66.1 per cent and the
State stands lowest in the overall ranking in female literacy. The decline
in child sex ratio and low female literacy rate in Rajasthan reflects the
underlying socio-economic conditions and deep-rooted cultural prejudices
against girls.

This calls for concerted action to combat the reasons behind the steep decline
in the overall sex ratio, particularly the child sex ratio. The Government of
India (Gol) and the Government of Rajasthan (GoR) have taken various
measures including the ones in the fields of health and nutrition and education,
to prevent discrimination against the girls both before and after their birth. A
number of schemes have been initiated and Acts passed to eliminate
discrimination and improve the health of girls and enhance the child sex ratio.
These are enumerated below:

e Pre-conception and Pre-natal Diagnostic Techniques (Prohibition of sex
selection) Act, 1994 (PC&PNDT Act), was enacted by Gol to act as
deterrent against misuse of such techniques for determination of sex of
foetus resulting in female foeticide. This Act was implemented in
Rajasthan with effect from (w.e.f.) 1 April 1996. Further, a scheme called

97


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

Mukhbir Yojana was also introduced by State Government w.e.f. 27
December 2011 for better implementation of the PC&PNDT Act.

Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006, enacted by Gol came into force
w.e.f. 1 November 2007, to shelter girls against early marriage and early
motherhood.

Kishori Shakti Yojana (KSY), a Centrally Sponsored Scheme was
introduced by Gol in the year 2000-01. The objective of this scheme was
to improve nutritional and health status of girls, to equip them to improve
and upgrade their home based vocational skills, and to promote their
overall development through activities at Anganwadi Centres (AWCs) and
Child Development Project Offices (CDPOs).

Rajiv Gandhi Scheme for Empowerment of Adolescent Girls or 'SABLA',
a new comprehensive scheme merging the KSY and Nutrition Programme
for Adolescent Girls (NPAG) was implemented from December 2010 in
10 districts*! of Rajasthan, to address the multi-dimensional problems of
adolescent girls. In the remaining districts, KSY continued as before.

Mukhya Mantri Balika Sambal Yojana was launched by State Government
w.e.f. 1 April 2007 to promote sterilisation after birth of one or two girl
children. Under this scheme, Unit Trust of India (UTI) bonds of € 10,000
are provided to each girl child of the spouse undergoing sterilisation after
one or two girl children. The objective of the scheme was to prevent the
decline in child sex ratio.

Apart from the above mentioned schemes and Acts, a couple of other
major schemes having important components focused on girls are
Integrated Child Development Services* and Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan
(SSA)*. A performance audit on ICDS scheme was included in the
Comptroller and Auditor General Report on Union Government (Civil),
Ministry of Women and Child Development (Report No.22 of 2012-13). A
Performance Audit on SSA has also been included at paragraph No. 2.1 of
this Report. As these schemes have been recently reviewed/included in the
current report, they have not been specifically included in this audit.

Audit examined the implementation of the various Acts and schemes
concerning the protection of girls and sought to ascertain whether they have
been appropriately funded and implemented effectively. For scrutiny of
implementation of schemes related to prevention of discrimination against
girls, nine districts were selected on random basis.

Audit findings have been divided into two sections namely ‘A’ & ‘B’. Gol has
passed two Acts namely the ‘Pre-conception and Pre-natal Diagnostic

Sriganganagar and Udaipur.

Integrated Child Development Services is to improve nutritional and health standards of
children and reduce the rate of child mortality, disease and malnutrition.

Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan is a flagship programme of Gol with the objective of

Techniques (Prohibition of Sex Selection) (PC&PNDT) Act, 1994’ and the
‘Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006’ for protection of girls. These issues
are dealt with in Section ‘A’, while Section ‘B’ covers the issues pertaining to
health and safety of girls’.

3.4.2.1 Implementation of the Pre-Conception and Pre-Natal Diagnostic
Techniques (Prohibition of Sex selection) Act, 1994

The PC&PNDT Act 1994, amended in 2003, is an important legislative
intervention aimed at preventing the decline in Child Sex Ratio (CSR). It
provides for the prohibition of sex selection, before or after conception and
regulation of prenatal diagnostic techniques for the prevention of their misuse
for sex determination leading to female foeticide. It requires registration of
Genetic Counselling Centres (GCC), Genetic Laboratories (GL) and Genetic
clinics (GC), prohibition on sale of ultrasound machines to persons,
laboratories not registered under the Act, etc. Each GC, ultrasound clinic,
imaging centre was required to maintain a record of pregnant women on
whom ultrasonography is conducted in form "F".

As per Section 17(2) of the Act the state government shall appoint one or more
Appropriate Authorities (AA) for the whole or part of the state for the purpose
of this Act having regard to the intensity of the problem of pre-natal sex
determination leading to female foeticide. A State Appropriate Authority
(SAA), District level Appropriate Authorities (DAA) and Sub Division Level
Appropriate Authorities (SDAA) were appointed (June/July 2001) by the State
Government, to grant/suspend/cancel registration of a GCC/GL/GC, to enforce
standards prescribed for the GCC, GL and GC, to investigate complaints of
breach of provisions of the Act, to take appropriate legal action against the use
of any sex selection technique by any person at any place, etc. The State
Advisory Committee was constituted in July 2001 for consideration of any
application for registration of any complaint for suspension or cancellation of
registration and to give advice thereon.

State Supervisory Board (SSB) was constituted in July 2003 under the
provisions of PC&PNDT Act. Functions of the Board are to monitor the
implementation of the Act, to create public awareness against the practice of
pre-conception sex selection and pre-natal determination of sex of foetus
leading to female foeticide in the State and to review the activities of the AAs
functioning in the State and recommend appropriate action, etc.

(a) Composition of Advisory Committees

State Government issued Notification (June 2001) to constitute Advisory
Committees at district and sub division levels comprising senior most
gynecologist, senior most pediatrician, medical geneticist, three eminent social
workers, district information and public relation officer and one legal expert
nominated by State Government.

Scrutiny of the records revealed that in test checked districts, State
Government did not nominate medical geneticist, eminent social workers and
legal expert; as such constitution of District and Sub Division Level Advisory

99


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

Committee remained incomplete (August 2014). Thus, required advice from
genetic, social and legal experts was not available to Appropriate Authorities
(DAAs and SDAAs).

(b) Constitution of PC&PNDT Bureau of Investigation

For constituting PC&PNDT Bureau of Investigation at State and District
Levels, Directorate of Medical Health and Family Welfare sanctioned
(December 2012) 18 posts*® at state level and 3 posts™® at district level. Of
these, six*’ employees at state level were posted in 2013 and only three of
them’*® were continuing as of August 2014. In the test-checked districts, it was
noticed that all the three sanctioned posts were lying vacant. Despite
PC&PNDT Act being in existence since 1996, the Bureau of Investigation was
not effectively operationalised with crucial posts lying vacant. Had the district
level Bureau of Investigation been operationalised with crucial posts being
filled up, it would have been effective in more stringent implementation of the
PC&PNDT Act and more successful decoy operations could have been
conducted as highlighted in sub-paragraph related to Mukhbir Yojana.

(० Inspection of sonography centres

As per Rules 11 and 12 of PC&PNDT Rules, 1996 the AA or officer
authorised in this behalf may enter and search at all reasonable times every
sonography centre and the sonography centre shall afford all reasonable
facilities for inspection of the place, equipment and records to the AAs.

The Directorate of Medical Health and Family Welfare fixed (February 2012)
monthly targets for District AAs and Sub-Divisional AAs for inspection of
sonography centres.

Scrutiny of records of selected districts revealed that requisite inspections of
sonography centres were not carried out during the period 2012-14. Shortfall
of inspections was as under:

In the selected nine districts, 110 cases were filed since 2006 and there was
only one case of conviction (where also an appeal is pending). In 18 cases,

The discrimination against girl starts even before she is born. In Rajasthan
there has been a marked decline in child sex ratio by 21 points in the Census
of 2011 from that of 2001. This decline highlights the importance of
implementing the Act in improving the child sex ratio and in taking pro-active
measures in changing the socio-cultural environment which would reduce
discrimination against girls.

(ad) Mukhbir Yojana

Mukhbirs are the informers of illegal use of sonography machines in the area
and such mukhbirs are suitably rewarded. They help the Directorate of
Medical Health and Family Welfare in conducting decoy operations at
sonography centres, suspected of violating the PC&PNDT Act.

In terms of the guidelines of the Mukhbir Yojana (started in December 2011),
% 50,000 would be given to an informer (Mukhbir) only after finding that his
information is true, followed by a successful decoy operation. Another
% 25,000 would be given to the informer after the charges against the person
involved in the violation of the Act is framed in the court of law. A further
amount of हैं 25,000 would be given to the Mukhbir when the accused is
proved guilty of the crime of sex selection.

It was noticed that during the period December 2011 to March 2014, only
seven successful decoy operations’ were conducted in Jaipur and first
instalment of € 3.25 lakh was disbursed. Of these, two cases have been filed in
Court by investigating agency and remaining five were still under
investigation even after lapse of more than one year. In the test-checked nine
districts, no decoy operations were conducted during this period. This could be
partially attributed to the Bureau of Investigation not functioning properly.

Thus, the Appropriate Authorities for enforcement of the Act were not notified
till June 2001 and nomination of members of district level Advisory
Committee could not be completed even after lapse of 18 years. PC&PNDT
Bureau of Investigation also did not come into force under the Act, which
shows that effective measures are not being taken by the State Government in
implementation of the Act.

Despite there being a marked decline in the child sex ratio in the State, the
PC&PNDT Act was barely implemented. Sonography centres were generally

% On 21 March 2012 (one), 9 November 2012 (one), 21 January 2013 (two), 2 May 2013

not inspected and the Mukhbir Yojana was also not very effective in curbing
the illegal detection of sex of unborn child.

3.4.2.2. Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006

Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006, was enacted by Gol in January 2007.
This legislation is aimed at prohibiting child marriages, protecting and
providing relief to victim and providing punishment for those who abet,
promote or solemnise such marriages. Child marriage is now considered a
violation of the human rights of the child and has extremely deleterious effect
on the health and wellbeing of the child.

As per the Status Report - Wings 2014 on ‘The World of India’s Girls’
published by Save the Children Organisation, 40 per cent of girls in Rajasthan
were getting married below the age of 18 years. This shows the widespread
prevalence of child marriages and need for taking pro-active measures to
implement the Prohibition of the Child Marriage Act.

(a) Follow-up action on complaints related to Child marriages

As per Rule 5 of the Rajasthan Prohibition of Child Marriage Rules 2007, the
Child Marriage Prohibition Officer (CMPO) on receipt of any complaint or
otherwise having got an information about solemnisation of a marriage in
violation of the Act, shall take appropriate action under Sections 4, 5 and 13 of
the Act. The CMPO for creating awareness of the evil which results from child
marriage and to sensitise the community on the issue of child marriage, may
seek assistance of any respectable member of the locality, an officer of the
Government or an office bearer of Non-government organisation.

As per information furnished by District authorities, against the reported 651
complaint cases in these test checked districts, 196 cases were found either
false or children were major. In 454 cases, marriage were stopped by the
respective CMPOs and in one case, matter was filed in court. However, test
check of the records of CMPOs posted at district headquarters in nine test
checked districts revealed that though complaints were being forwarded to
concerned police station, tehsildars and programme officers of Women
Empowerment Department for preventive action, but nothing was found on
record regarding creating community awareness about the social evil of child
marriages.

(b) Provisions under the Act not being followed

Under section 16(1) of the Act, State Government notified (November 2007)
all the Sub-Divisional Officers as Child Marriage Prohibition Officer (CMPO)
for their sub-divisions. As per Rule 4 of Rajasthan Prohibition of Child
Marriage Rules 2007, the CMPO would, from time to time, inspect the records
of Marriage Registration Officers under his jurisdiction and assess that no
child marriage has taken place. In case any child marriage is noticed, he will
take action as provided in the Act against the persons responsible for the same.
During scrutiny of records of CMPOs, posted at district headquarters, in all

102


Chapter III Compliance Audit

nine test checked districts, it was noticed that records of ‘Marriage
Registration Offices’ were not being inspected by CMPOs.

The Government stated (December 2014) that letter to this effect have been
issued to all District Magistrate for inspection of records of marriage
registration offices and initiate action as per rules.

In this section, audit examined the scope and implementation of the nutrition
based programmes and the conditional cash transfer schemes, implemented by
the Government for girls.

3.4.2.3 Kishori Shakti Yojana (KSY)

The objective of this scheme was to improve nutritional and health status of
girls, to equip them to improve and upgrade their home based vocational
skills, and to promote their overall development under two sub Schemes.
Scheme-I has been designed for adolescent girls in the age group of 11 to 15
years belonging to poor families whose annual income is below % 6,400 per
annum. Scheme-II is intended to reach to all adolescent girls in the age group
of 11 to 18 years irrespective of income levels of the family. Younger girls in
the age group of 11 to 15 years and belonging to poor families got a definite
preference in the identification process even under Scheme-II.

Under this scheme supplementary nutrition is to be provided to two adolescent
girls at each AWC having 20 to 25 adolescent girls irrespective of actual
number of malnourished girls identified. Supplementary nutrition is to be
provided at the scale (© five per day/beneficiary) as provided to pregnant and
lactating mothers under ICDS.

KSY was implemented in all the 33 Districts of the State. From 2011-12 it was
replaced by SABLA in 10 districts*', and continued in 35,646 AWCs (190
projects) in the remaining 23 Districts, of which 6 districts"? with 10,629
AWCSs (59 projects) were selected for review in audit.

(a) Supplementary nutrition to adolescent girls

As per scheme guidelines, two malnourished girls, from each AWC, were to
be identified in the age group of 11-15 years for providing 500 calories of
energy and 20 grams of protein (supplementary nutrition) as meal for six days
in a week. The nutrition component was to be provided from the Integrated
Child Development Services (ICDS) project.

It was noticed that supplementary nutrition (premix upma and halwa) was not
provided for periods ranging from 4 to 36 months to two identified girls in test
checked Baran, Sirohi and Nagaur districts during the period 2011-14
(Appendix 3.2).

“Baran, Chittorgarh, Jhunjhunu, Nagaur, Sikar and Sirohi.

103


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

provided under this scheme with that provided under the mid-day meal
scheme it is seen that under the Mid-Day Meal scheme 150 gram food grains
(700 calories) along with cooking conversion cost of हैं 5.38 per day per
beneficiary, is provided, apart from the cost of infrastructure for centralised
kitchens.

State Government accepted (December 2014) that supplementary nutrition
was not distributed in Baran district while it was distributed in Nagaur district
after November 2011. However necessary directions have been issued to all
districts for regular distribution of supplementary nutrition.

(b) Lack of vocational training and exposure visits

As per scheme guidelines (para 10.4), two selected adolescent girls from each
AWC were to be trained for 60 days in vocational skills*! to equip them to
train other girls of the AWC for improving home based and vocational skills.
Guidelines also provided for exposure visit of each girl to places like post
office, bank, panchayat, hospital etc. to acquaint them with the important
facilities available in the area.

(i) Scrutiny of records revealed that vocational training was not provided
by department to enrolled adolescent girls in AWCs of Nagaur and Sirohi
districts during the period 2011-14 and in Chittorgarh district during the
period 2011-12 and 2013-14.

State Government accepting (December 2014) the facts stated that in Sirohi
district, vocational training could not be conducted due to posts of field
functionaries being vacant while in Chittorgarh district vocational training
during 2013-14 could not be conducted due to non allocation of funds by Gol.

all CDPOs of Baran, it was seen that 318 girls in 133 AWCs of eight ICDS
projects were provided vocational training for 2 to 3 days only during the
period 2012-13 by Krishi Vigyan Kendra Anta (NGO) against a period of 60
days as required under the scheme. Further, after getting vocational training,
these two selected adolescent girls, were unable to provide training to the
remaining adolescent girls of AWCs. Fact remains that vocational training was
mostly not provided in the six test checked districts.

State Government replied (December 2014) that the guidelines issued (May
2011) by the Director, Women Empowerment, chose an option according to
availability of funds and left it at the discretion of the districts to conduct the
vocational training programme. Reply is not in conformity with the guidelines
issued by Gol.

Exposure visit were not conducted in Chittorgarh district during
2012-14, and in Sirohi district in 2013-14. State Government replied

(० Short utilisation of budget allocation

As per paragraph 12 and 13 of guidelines, the State Governments may prepare
a plan of action under the scheme and the interventions may be limited to the
approved amount of € 1.10 lakh per block/ICDS project per annum only.

190 projects under KSY are functioning in Rajasthan. Hence the requisite
funds to be allotted amount to हैं 2.09 crore. Against this हैं 3.53 crore” were
available in the year 2011-12. Expenditure of € 1.32 crore only was incurred
during the year leaving an unspent balance of € 2.21 crore.

Despite availability of funds, action plans for the period 2012-14 were not
prepared by the Department of Women and Child Development and therefore
Gol did not allocate any amount during this period. Thus, no activities were
conducted during the years 2012-14 under KSY.

State Government accepted (December 2014) the facts and stated that due to
vacant posts and paucity of staff, some activities of the action plan could not
be reflected. However, Gol has not allotted any budget for the KSY during
financial year 2012-14, hence there was no need to develop action plan for the
above mentioned years.

Reply was not in conformity with Paragraph 12 of guidelines which stipulates
for preparation of Plan of Action under the KSY and sharing it with Gol.

Thus, in Kishori Shakti Yojana (KSY) the objective of vocational training,
exposure visit, livelihood training, could not be achieved due to non-allotment
of budget during 2012-13 to 2013-14. Extending of nutrition benefits to only
two poor malnourished girls severely limits the scope of the scheme in view of
prevalence of malnutrition and anemia among adolescent girls. Thus, the
nutrition component requires revision. Further the norms of % 5/- per day per
head were fixed in December 2008 and requires review.

3.4.2.4 Rajiv Gandhi Scheme for Empowerment of Adolescent Girls
(SABLA)

The objectives of the scheme were to enable self development and
empowerment of adolescent girls; improve their nutrition and health status;
spread awareness about ‘Adolescent Reproductive and Sexual Health’ and
family and child care; upgrade their home-based skills, life skills and
vocational skills; introduce mainstream out-of-school adolescent girls into
formal/non- formal education; and inform and guide them about existing
public services. SABLA was launched in 10 districts in Rajasthan. In these
districts, SABLA was to replace KSY.

* Opening balance € 2.48 crore plus funds released 1.05 crore

105


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

The main difference between KSY and SABLA is that nutrition was to be
provided to all girls under SABLA against only two poor malnourished girls
under KSY.

(a) Short release of funds

As per budget provision under SABLA, a sum of % 3.80 lakh was to be
allocated for each ICDS project by Gol towards cost of non-nutrition
component*’, In addition, supplementary nutrition @% 5 per adolescent girl
per day for 300 days in a year was to be provided on actual basis. As such,
Gol was required to release हैं 4.33 crore for 114 ICDS projects of ten districts
for non-nutritional part of the scheme and for nutrition part as per actual
requirement. During the year 2012-13, Gol sanctioned and released हैं 3.10
crore against the required funds of हैं 4.33 crore for the non-nutritional
component. Similarly, during the year 2013-14, Gol sanctioned हैं 4.36 crore
but released हैं 2.18 crore only. Thus, the required funds to run the activities as
per guidelines were not provided by Gol.

State Government stated (December 2014) that sanction for second instalment
(₹ 2.18 crore) of 2013-14 was received by the department on 6 May 2014 as
such the same was got revalidated from Gol for financial year 2014-15.

As per paragraph 6.1.1 of guidelines, supplementary nutrition may be

adolescent girls at the rate of 5 per beneficiary per day, for 300 days in a
year.

(9 Lack of Vocational Training

(i) As per paragraph 6.8 of guidelines, vocational training is to be

Thus, the benefit of SABLA was not extended to all the eligible girls due to
non enrolment of 31 to 55 per cent of girls. Out of enrolled girls 9 to 45 per
cent could not be extended benefit of Nutrition. Of these, in two test checked
districts, vocational training also was not provided. Further, training kits were
also not supplied in any districts during the period 2012-13 resulting into non-
achievement of objectives of the scheme.

(०) Maintenance of Kishori Cards

Under SABLA Yojana, a card, to be called Kishori Card (KC), for each
adolescent girl would be maintained at AWC. This card would contain
information like weight, height, body mass index (BMI), IFA
supplementation, referrals and services received under SABLA. The card
would also contain important milestones in the girl’s life like joining and
leaving school, marriage, etc.

In the three test-checked districts, it was noticed that KCs were not maintained
for any adolescent girl. In absence of KCs, benefits provided to each
beneficiary under SABLA scheme could not be ascertained.

107


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

State Government accepted (December 2014) that the cards during the period
2012-13 and 2013-14 could not be printed and distributed due to non-receipt
of second instalment from Gol.

3.4.2.6 Mukhya Mantri Balika Sambal Yojana

As per guidelines of scheme, implemented since 2007 the Directorate of
Medical Health and Family Welfare (DMH&FW), would deposit a sum of
₹ 10,000 in UTI bonds under Children Career Plan in the name of each girl
child up to 5 years of age, of the couple undergoing sterilisation after birth of
one or two female children only. Maturity amount of UTI bond was payable to
the girl subject to the condition that she was not married before attaining the
age of 18 years. Application for bonds would be submitted to Additional/
Deputy Chief Medical and Health Officer (Add./Dy. CMHO)-Family Welfare
of the district, who would forward the application to Additional Director,
Family Welfare on 5“and 20" of the month. On receipt of application from
Additional Director (Family Welfare), UTI would issue bonds in favour of the

109



Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014
girl child and forward to Addl./Dy. CMHO (Family Welfare) for distribution
to the beneficiaries.

Scrutiny of records in test checked districts revealed that:

. No record to identify number of beneficiaries eligible under the

scheme was maintained to ensure that the benefit was being extended to all
eligible beneficiaries.

. No record of receipt of application was maintained in the offices of
ACMHOs.
. In the test checked districts, out of 444 applications forwarded by

ACMHOs to AD (FW), Jaipur during 2011-14, 283“ applications were
forwarded with a delay of more than 6 to 74 months from the date of
sterilisation, with consequent delay in issue and receipt of bonds indicating
that authorities were not serious in implementing the scheme.

. For applications of 2,916 beneficiaries received during 2007-14,

Chapter III Compliance Audit

3.4.2.7. Conclusions and Recommendation

The Government of India and the State Government have taken various
measures to remove discrimination against girls. Accordingly, a couple of
Acts have been passed to eliminate discrimination against the girls at birth and
abolish child marriages. It was seen that the implementation of the PC&PNDT
Act has been ineffective as shown by the steep decline in the Child Sex Ratio.
There was also shortfall in inspection of sonography centres. Further
PC&PNDT Bureau of Investigation was not operationalised with crucial posts
lying vacant. The Prohibition of Child Marriage Act has not had much impact
in delaying the marriage age. The implementation of both these Acts requires
that the State Government take pro-active and affirmative action to bring
about a change in the cultural milieu in which children especially girls, are
raised.

The Government needs to take effective steps for rigorous implementation of
PC&PNDT Act, specifically in the areas of inspection of sonography centres
and operationalisation of PC&PNDT Bureau of Investigation.

Under both the KSY and SABLA schemes, nutritional benefits @ हैं 5 per day
per beneficiary were provided which is insufficient. Under mid day meal
scheme 150 gram food grains (700 calories) along with cooking conversion
cost of हैं 5.38, per day per beneficiary, is provided. Further, the nutrition
benefit was not provided to a large proportion of adolescent girls under KSY.

State Government needs to review the coverage of beneficiaries under KSY.
The merging of KSY and SABLA needs to be considered as both the
schemes address similar issues. The cost limit fixed for providing nutrition
to girls needs to be reviewed due to prevalence of malnutrition and anemia
amongst adolescent girls in the State.

3.4.3 Construction of Sports Infrastructure/Stadium

Sports and games promote good health and spirit of friendly competition
which creates a positive impact on the overall personality development of
youth and society. Sports and games are also an effective way of channeling
the energy of youth for productive and meaningful purposes. Sports is a State
subject enumerated (Entry 33) in list II (State list), Seventh Schedule of the
Constitution of India and thus the State Government has exclusive powers to
make laws with respect to sports (with the support of Government of India

(Gol).

In terms of National Sports Policy 2001, the Central Government in
conjunction with the State Government, the Olympic Association and the
National Sports Federation will concertedly pursue the twin objectives of
‘broadbasing’ of sports and ‘achieving excellence’ in sports at National and
International levels. The State Sports Policy, launched in April 2013 envisages
inter alia, development of a suitable sports environment and infrastructure for

111


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

maximum attraction of children towards sports, increasing the participation of
the players in national and international tournaments and providing facilities
for best performances.

Though population wise, Rajasthan stands at eighth position in the country as
per Census 2011, but it scored at 20" rank in 347 National Games 2011. This
indicates that there is huge scope of improvement in sport activities in the
State.

All sports activities including creation and promotion of infrastructure,
encouragement to players and promoting a sports environment as envisaged in
State Sports Policy (April 2013) are planned by the Youth Affairs and Sports
Department (YA&SD), Government of Rajasthan, and implemented by the
Rajasthan State Sports Council (RSSC). An organogram depicting the various
authorities in-charge of management and execution of sports in the state and
districts is as under:

Secretary RSSC
v

The funds for sports are allocated under three schemes. Two schemes are
centrally sponsored viz. Panchayat Yuva Krida Aur Khel Abhiyan (PYKKA)
and Urban Sport Infrastructure Scheme (USIS)). One scheme ‘Integrated
Stadium (Sports Infrastructure) Development Programme (ISDP), 2007’ is run
by the Government of Rajasthan. USIS and ISDP are implemented by District
Sports Councils (DSCs)/ District Sports Officers (DSOs) in the districts, while
PYKKA is being implemented by Zila Parishad, Block Panchayat/Village
Panchayats.

Audit reviewed the implementation of the above three schemes in 12
districts’ (out of 33 districts of seven divisions) covering a varying

45° Ajmer, Alwar, Baran, Bharatpur, Bikaner, Chittorgarh, Hanumangarh, Jaipur, Jodhpur,

Kota, Nagaur and Udaipur.

112


Chapter III Compliance Audit

geographical diversity. The implementation of these schemes was test checked
for the period 2009-10 to 2013-14.

Scrutiny (April-July 2014) of the records of the RSSC and DSCs of the test
checked districts alongwith the records of executing agencies ie. Public
Works Department (PWD), Rajasthan Avas Vikas Infrastructure Ltd.
(RAVIL) and Rajasthan State Road Development and Construction
Corporation (RSRDCC), revealed following observations:

3.

1 Non-constitution of Sports Council

As per Article-2 of Memorandum of RSSC, a 21 member Sports Council
should be constituted with a President, Vice- President, Treasurer, six official
ex-officio members and not more than twelve other members, out of whom at
least one should be woman representative. The aims and objectives of the
council were to plan and promote all round development of games and sports,
to organise and impart training, coaching and education in sports and games
and to establish and maintain infrastructure facilities for the same through
monitoring the execution of construction works of stadiums, play grounds etc.

The RSSC, constituted in May 2001, was withdrawn in January 2004 by State
Government. Three Government officials** were nominated to look after the
work of council till the formation of a new council. It was also noted that
memorandum of RSSC did not prescribe representation from various sports as
members of council. It was also observed that even after a lapse of more than
ten years, there was no duly constituted Sports Council (RSSC) with
representatives from the sports bodies, and the Council was being exclusively
managed by Government officials nominated by State Government.

The Council stated (September 2014) that the RSSC was to be constituted by
State Government and they had requested them to constitute the RSSC
(September 2014). Action from State Government is awaited (November
2014).

Likewise, there should be a District Sports Council (DSC) with maximum 307
nominated members in every district. All the DSCs were dissolved in
February 2004 and are yet to be re-constituted. In absence of the
representation from public representatives, Education Department,
sportspersons, women etc. in DSCs, the very purpose to capture the
perspective of various stakeholders, including sportspersons, in planning and
promoting all-round development of sports could not be achieved.

It is recommended that to bring representative character of sports persons on
board and to make RSSC run on professional lines, the RSSC memorandum

* Secretary of the YA&SD: President; Deputy Secretary of the YA&SD: Vice President and

Assistant Accounts Officer of the YA&SD: Treasurer in the department.
30 nominated members should be among Zila Pramukh, MLA, Pradhan, Superintendent
of Police, Chairman (Nagar Palika, Council), District Education Officer, active sports
participants (11), representative of District Sports Association (1), Talented sports men
(2), Women (2), representative of SC/ST/OBC (3).

113


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

should be duly amended, to prescribe representation from various sports
bodies as member of the council.

3.4.3.2 Integrated Stadium (Sports Infrastructure) Development
Programme (ISDP), 2007

ISDP 2007 was launched by the State Government with the objective of
creating basic sports infrastructure, preserving existing play fields and
developing suitable play grounds, stadiums etc. at Division, District and
Teshsil levels. ISDP is funded by State Government as well as by mobilising
resources from Gol, Local Bodies, Corporate Houses, Sports Association ete.
A proportionate amount equal to 10 per cent of sanction is desirable to be
contributed by Member of Parliament/Member of Legislative Assembly Local
Area Development (MP LAD /MLA LAD) funds or other local sources.

Some of the major sports projects which could not be taken up due to
procedural and other delays led to deprivation of sports person from practicing
on modern tracks/ grounds as discussed below:

(0) Lack of sports infrastructure in the State

As per the ISDP norms, at division level, facilities of outdoor stadium with 10
disciplines“ besides one indoor stadium, should be provided while at district
level, facilities of outdoor stadium with 9 disciplines” besides one indoor
stadium needs to be provided. The ISDP guidelines also envisaged that the
State Government should construct stadia in a time bound manner in three
years from 2007 onwards.

Examination by audit revealed that even after a lapse of six years, five
divisional and 26 districts stadia have not been provided with adequate
sports/infrastructure facilities (June 2014). Reason put forth by the RSSC was
shortage of funds. There was thus a substantial shortfall in the availability of
sports infrastructure in the State. Despite this, no time bound action plan was
prepared to provide adequate infrastructure.

The Council stated (September 2014) that sports infrastructure/facilities are
created only after receiving sanction of State Government. Fact remains that
YA&SD did not prepare a time bound programme to develop sports facilities
in the State.

It was also seen that the RSSC has not created any manual/computerised data-
base of sports infrastructure. It was informed that entries of constructed sports
infrastructure facility are being made in the assets registers maintained by
Stadium Supervisor. However, the register was found incomplete.

(ii) Under ISDP, RSSC released हैं 55.41 crore against the sanctioned
amount of % 64.59 crore for 89 works of construction of stadiums, creation

Athletics (400 meters), football, hockey, handball, basketball, volleyball, tennis, kho-kho,
kabaddi and swimming pool.

and kabaddi.

(iii) Blocking of funds

Construction of Synthetic Track at Gaushala Sports Complex in Jodhpur at a
cost of % five crore was declared in Budget 2012-13. For this work, provision
of % three crore was made in 2012-13. The Department issued directions (May
2012) to RSSC for completion of the work by 30 June 2013. State
Government transferred (December 2012) % three crore to the PD account of
RSSC, which was further transferred to the executing agency Jodhpur
Development Authority (JDA), in April 2013. Tenders were invited by JDA
on 15 April 2013. Technical bid was opened on 17 May 2013 but not
evaluated within the validity period of 90 days for accepting tenders and
issuance of work order which resulted in lapsing of the bids. JDA had to
cancel the tender process and reinvite tenders in February 2014, which were
not finalised as of October 2014. Thus, there was undue delay in finalising the
tender process and & three crore remained blocked with JDA since April 2013.
Construction of synthetic track could not be started. Escalation of cost of the
work can also not be ruled out.

The State Government stated (October 2014) that the action related to
finalisation of tender is under process with JDA and disciplinary action against
officer responsible for delay is also to be initiated by JDA. Reasons for not
evaluating the bids in time were asked for from JDA (September 2014). Reply
from JDA is awaited.

(0). Non-construction of Velodrome

A cycle velodrome was constructed at Karni Singh Stadium, Bikaner in 1980.
In absence of proper maintenance and repair, velodrome became dilapidated
and was rendered unusable by the cyclists.

फ़

Cycle velodrome (Dr. Karni Singh Stadium, Bikaner) in a dilapidated condition

YA&SD sanctioned (July 2008) a grant of % one crore for construction of a
new velodrome through RAVIL. RSSC which was assigned the work of
selecting a site, recommended (April 2009) repair of the existing velodrome
through PWD instead of constructing a new one. Due to procedural delay,
money was released (May 2014) to DSC, Bikaner after a delay of more than
five years. The work is yet to be started. This resulted in non-repairing of the
velodrome and depriving the cyclists from practicing for National and
International tournaments.

as in absence of approach road and boundary wall proper utilisation and safety
of wrestling hall constructed remains to be addressed.

issued by the Department to PWD, therefore the matter lies between
administrative department and PWD. This shows lack of co-ordination and
communication between RSSC, department and PWD. Decision to drop

3.4.3.3 Urban Sports Infrastructure Scheme (USIS)

USIS, a comprehensive scheme for supporting the urban sports infrastructure,
was introduced by Gol in 2010-11. The scheme aimed at addressing the entire
‘sports eco system’ holistically, namely players training, coaching and
development of infrastructure, initially for two years on a pilot basis
(2010-12). The scheme envisaged development of community play fields,
coaching and coach development programme and players academies. It inter
alia, provided development of one project in a year by each State, with
maximum financial assistance of % six crore. Remaining cost was to be borne
by the State Government.

During the period 2010-11 to 2012-13, three works of construction of ‘Multi
Purpose Indoor Hall’ at Jodhpur, Karauli and Alwar were sanctioned under
USIS on submission of proposal by State Government. Against the sanctioned
amount of € 18 crore, an expenditure of € 5.88 crore was incurred (May 2014)

3.4.3.4 Panchayat Yuva Krida Aur Khel Abhiyan (PYKKA)

PYKKA was launched (May 2008) by Gol to provide universal access and
promote a sports culture among both boys and girls and to harness available
and potential sporting talent among rural population by providing basic sports

119


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 Marc!

(7) Non-submission of UCs resulting in short receipt of grant

As per PYKKA guidelines 10 per cent village panchayats and block
panchayats were to be covered under the scheme during 2008-09 and
2009-10. For the year 2009-10, Gol released रैं 2.75 crore as first instalment
against the sanctioned amount of & 7.82 crore for 917 village panchayats and
25 block panchayats. As per condition of sanction, UCs should have to be
submitted within 12 months of the closure of the financial year. However,
RSSC did not submit UCs for the year 2008-09. This resulted in non-release of
the remaining central grant of हैं 5.07 crore of 2009-10 by Gol and
corresponding contribution of 1.69 crore™’ by State Government.

Accepting the facts, State Government stated (October 2014) that UCs have
been sent to Gol in September 2014. However, details of such UCs were not
provided.

(ii) Non-coverage of panchayats resulting in non-receipt of Gol grant

As per PYKKA guidelines, basic sports infrastructure and equipment at the
panchayat level were to be provided. Accordingly, 5,822 village panchayats
and 159 block panchayats were to be covered during the period 2008-09 to
2013-14. However, only 1,786 village panchayats and 49 block panchayats
were covered during 2008-09 to 2009-10 and no coverage was done thereafter.
Thus, there was a short fall in coverage of 4,036 village panchayats (69 per cent)
and 110 block panchayats (69 per cent). This resulted in non receipt of
central grant of % 34.40 crore.

State Government stated (October 2014) that though the proposals for sanction
of grant for the year 2010-11 to 2012-13 were sent to Gol but the grant was
not released due to non-submission of UCs. However, details of such
proposals were not furnished.

amount of grant-in-aid was to be utilised only for the purpose for which it was

sanctioned and unspent balance of grant-in-aid if any, was to be refunded
immediately after completion of the project.

3.4.3.5 Financial Management by RSSC

The RSSC, being the nodal agency for sports infrastructure development,
receives all grants-in-aid from Gol and State Government. The grants from
State Government are credited in its non-interest bearing Personal Deposit
(PD) account. Grant in aid under PYKKA and USIS are directly received by

121


Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

RSSC in its saving bank account’!. RSSC is also maintaining separate bank
accounts™ for other miscellaneous transactions (receipts like fees, rents etc.
and payments of administrative expenditures). However, year-wise details of
grants-in-aids received and expenditure made against each grant were not
made available by the RSSC despite repeated requests (April 2014-July 2014).

Rule 5 and 48 (v) of General Financial and Accounts Rules (GF&ARs)
provides that all money received by or on behalf of Government shall be
remitted into bank account.

During scrutiny of records of RSSC, Jaipur, it was noticed that amounts
ranging between & 14.49 lakh and हैं 46.59 lakh, received on account of tender
fee, rent of stadium, income from swimming pool, miscellaneous and other
receipts during 2008-09 to 2012-13, were not credited to the bank account of
RSSC and kept as cash. The cash was utilised for payments against
expenditures on purchases, advances, traveling allowances, meetings,

Keeping heavy cash balances and utilising it without depositing in bank, was
in contravention of said Rules. Surprise physical verification of cash was also
not being carried out. Information regarding filling of Fidelity Guarantee
Policy by cashier was not produced to audit (September 2014). In the absence
of these checks, the possibility of utilisation of money for unauthorised
purposes and loss due to mishandling/mishappening, could not be ruled out.

3.4.3.6 Conclusions

The audit scrutiny revealed large gaps in the sports infrastructure provided in
the State. The State Sports Council and District Sports Councils were not
constituted since 2004, in absence of which opinion of public representatives,
Education and Police Departments, sports persons, women members, etc.,

There was slow progress in upgrading/construction of sports infrastructure.
The RSSC and YA&SD were not monitoring the physical and financial

Non-recovery of € 85.30 lakh from the licensee for testing fewer number
of free cases and extending undue monetary benefit.

Member Secretary, Rajasthan Medicare Relief Society (RMRS)-cum- Medical
Superintendent, Sawai Man Singh (SMS) Hospital, Jaipur entered into an
agreement (July 2007) with M/s Imaging Super Consultant Private Limited
(licensee) Jaipur for installation and running of 'Linear Accelerator Machine!
meant for treating cancer patients in SMS Hospital. The period of agreement
was ten years from the date of the commissioning/operation (28 April 2009) of
the facility. As per clause 7 of the agreement, 21.21 per cent share out of the
total revenue collected would have to be paid by the licensee to RMRS every
month. Clause 8 ibid, enjoined upon licensee to extend free treatment to
patients, up to 20 per cent of cases referred by hospital authorities. If number
of free cases were more than the mandatory 20 per cent, RMRS would pay for
the excess and if the free cases were less than 20 per cent, the licensee would
pay back the money to RMRS for the shortage every six months. No clause
existed in the agreement for levying interest in the event of failure of timely
payment of Government dues by the licensee.

Test-check (January-February 2014) of records of SMS Hospital, Jaipur
revealed that the licensee carried out tests in 5,457 cases referred by the
hospital authorities between 2009-10 and 2012-13 for eight modalities
(tests)**, of which only 666 cases were referred as free cases to the licensee.
This was 425 less than the mandatory 20 per cent (1,091 cases) required to be
tested free. The licensee paid RMRS’ share (21.21 per cent) of = 1.83 crore
from the revenue of & 8.63 crore collected for 4,791 tests (5457 - 666), but he
did not pay = 90.30 lakh for short testing of 425 free cases at the rates

Examination of records of SMS Hospital, Jaipur by audit revealed that in a

State Government stated (September 2014) that the licensee has admitted only

clearly stipulated in clause 8 of the agreement that in case the free cases
referred by the hospital, are less than 20 per cent, the licensee will pay back
the money to RMRS for the shortage every six months. The fact remained that
the balance amount of & 85.30 lakh was not recovered from the licensee till
date (August 2014).

Thus, the hospital authorities failed to recover हैं 85.30 lakh from the licensee
for testing less number of free cases despite lapse of more than one to four
years thereby extending undue monetary benefit to the licensee. Moreover, in
absence of any provision for charging interest, the Government could not levy
the same on delayed payment.

Blockage of funds of ¥ 17.57 crore for nearly three years and loss of
interest of € 1.99 crore thereon due to non-execution of work of 33 KV
dedicated power feeder line.

Policy Planning Committee (PPC) of Rajasthan Water Supply and Sewerage
Management Board (RWSSMB) of Public Health Engineering Department
(PHED) issued (August 2006) administrative and financial (A&F) sanction
@ 761.01 crore) for Nagaur Lift Water Supply Project to benefit a population
of 502 villages and 5 towns. Technical sanction was issued (August 2006) for
% 630.72 crore. The project was divided into seven packages* each one to be
taken up as per availability of budget.

Test-check (February 2013) of records of Executive Engineer (EE), PHED,
Nagaur Lift Project Division-I, Nagaur and further information collected
(December 2013), revealed that while the civil work” under package 1 had
been completed (January 2012), the works under packages 2, 3 and 4 were
under progress, work under packages 5 and 6 were yet to be undertaken and
work of package 7 was proposed to be got done as a deposit work”. The
sanction of the project included construction of separate 132K V/33KV power
feeder systems and 33 KV power feeder line under packages 1, 2, 3 and 4.
This work was completed for package 1, was under progress for package 2 and
3 and not yet allotted for package 4.

Scrutiny of records revealed that:

. Under package 3, EE, PHED, Nagaur requested (June 2011) Ajmer
Vidyut Vitaran Nigam Limited (AVVNL) for laying of 33K V dedicated power
feeder lines. While accepting the request, the EE, AVVNL issued (June 2011)
a consolidated demand notice of % 27.95 crore (Package 2: % 10.38 crore and
Package 3: % 17.57 crore). The amount was paid (June 2011) from the savings

up at the higher level. It was further added that loss calculated by audit seems
hypothetical as the advance demanded by AVVNL was based on the prevalent
rates at the relevant time (June 2011). The demand would have been much
higher had the same been made at present rates. Hence, the assessment of loss
does not appear correct.

Reply is not acceptable as AVVNL has still not started the work and the
amount of advance paid to AVVNL remains blocked without any productive
use resulting in loss of interest. Moreover, the argument that the advance was
based on the prevalent rates at the relevant time does not hold good as delay in
execution of the work will ultimately result in cost escalation and the
Government will have to pay the differential amount to AVVNL.

Social Justice and Empowerment Department (SJED), Government of
Rajasthan, framed (April 2007) ‘Government and Non-Government Colleges,

“National Rural Drinking Water Programme is a centrally sponsored programme under

39



Chapter III Compliance Audit

Secondary and Senior Secondary Schools Aided Hostels Operating Rules
2007’ for aided hostels in Government or Non-Government Colleges,
Secondary and Senior Secondary Schools (Educational Institutions), solely for
the students belonging to Scheduled Castes/Scheduled Tribes, children of
Safaiwalas and Denotified Tribes. SIED amended (September 2007) these
Rules by inserting Sub Rule 11 under Rule 15, which provided for
construction of hostels in such Educational Institutions. The amount of grant
for construction of each hostel was fixed ” up to a maximum of & 40 lakh. The
Heads of the Institution, where construction of hostel building had been
approved by SJED on the recommendation of District Officer, were required
to start construction within 60 days from the date of issue of sanction. The
grant was to be released at various stages of the construction work in three
installments".

Rule 5(3) of the Operating Rules envisaged recovery of the grant, in case any
educational institution failed to operate the hostel. Rule 10 (1) envisaged
granting of € 400/- per student per month for food and other amenities. In case
this amount was found insufficient by any Institution, it may arrange the extra
funds either from donors or from the students.

Test Check (October 2013) of records of Commissioner, SJED, Jaipur
revealed that SJED issued financial sanctions of % 9.13 crore between
December 2007 and April 2008 for construction of one hostel building each in
50 Educational Institutions. Of these, 10 hostel buildings were completed and
made operational during 2012-14 while 2 institutions (Pilibanga and Jhadol)
returned the funds (¥ 0.26 crore) to the Department.

Of the remaining 38 hostels, building work was also completed between June
2008 and March 2011 by 35 Institutions after incurring an expenditure of
₹ 6.76 crore but the same were not made operational. Six of these Institutions
did not obtain recognition certificates for running the hostels while 29
expressed their inability to run the hostels at हैं 400/- per student per month.
Three Institutions did not complete the buildings as of March 2014 even after
incurring an expenditure of € 0.21 crore.

It is noticed that 80 percent of hostels (40 out of 50) could not be made
operational even more than six years after the funds were sanctioned. The
target group being students belonging to deprived sections of the society, it
suggests that a thorough feasibility study was not undertaken while allocating
only = 400 per student per month. Since the rules were framed keeping in
view providing hostel accommodation to the students of socially and
economically weaker sections, the Government should also consider the
limited power of such students to compensate the additional amount in excess
of = 400 per month. As intention of the Government is a developmental one or

oo

% 10 lakh for a unit (hostel) with a capacity of 25 students; हैं 20 lakh for 50 students;
% 30 lakh for 75 students and 40 lakh for 100 and above.

30 per cent on completion of plinth level work, 50 per cent on completion of roof level
work and 20 per cent after completion certificate is issued by Technical Officer.

Rule 15 (3) provides for recognition for running the hostel for three years by SJED on
receipt of recommendation from respective District Officers.

Audit Report (G&SS) for the year ended 31 March 2014

a welfare measure, merely closing down the hostels or recovering the allocated
funds is no solution to the problem nor is it going to serve the purpose.
Therefore, the Government may consider coming up with an amendment to
increase the monthly grant of € 400 per month so that it may match the current
rate of expenditure to some sort.

Thus, 38 hostels constructed/not completed at a cost of f 6.97 crore, remained
inoperational, rendering the expenditure unfruitful. More than the expenditure,
the social objective of providing residential accommodation to SC/ST and
other students was defeated. As majority of Heads of the Institutions did not
make any effort to arrange for the funds either from donors or students and
were not willing to run the hostels, the grant was required to be recovered
from these Institutions under Rule 5 (3) ibid. However, the department did not
recover the amount so far (June 2014).

State Government, while accepting the facts, stated (October 2014) that
recognition of six hostels could not be granted due to non-submission of
proposal by the institutions to the department and lack of recommendation of
District Officers. Instructions are being issued to these institutions for running
of hostels. Further, instructions have also been issued to these institutions for
depositing the amount and effective measure will be taken to recover the
amount.

Thus, the Government failed to operationalise hostels six years after sanction
and the amount sanctioned per student was not sufficient which resulted in
failure of the scheme.

Non-utilisation of Special Central Assistance of = 9.36 crore and
deprivation of tribal students from job opportunities due to non-
observance of procedure in selecting the implementing agency.

Special Central Assistance (SCA) to Tribal Sub-Plan (TSP) is an additive to
the TSP of the State and is meant for filling up the critical gaps to cover
employment-cum-income generation activities and to raise the socio-economic
level of scheduled tribes.

During test check (October 2013) of records of Commissioner, Tribal Area
Development Department (TAD), Government of Rajasthan, Udaipur it was
observed that ‘Acetel Technologies’ (Acetel), an agency engaged in imparting
training on telecommunication, submitted (February 2011) its proposal for the
programme ‘skill enhancement training and deployment for tribal students’
directly to the State Government which was forwarded (February 2011) to the
Commissioner, TAD, for examination. The Additional Commissioner (AC),

128


Chapter III Compliance Audit

TAD, noticed (4 March 2011) several shortcomings® in this proposal and
advised the State Government to forward it to Gol after making necessary
amendments. Instead of addressing the shortcomings pointed out by AC and
following the proper selection procedure, State Government submitted (March
2011) the Acetel’s proposal of € 15.60 crore to the Government of India
(Gol) for providing training under TSP, to 8,000 tribal students @ % 19,500/-
per student. Gol, issued financial sanction (March 2011) of € 9.36 crore for
4,800 tribal students, with the condition that the State Government would
ensure 100 per cent placement of the trainees and would select an
implementing agency by observing transparent procedure and relevant
financial rules“. However, approval of implementing agency (Acetel), as
proposed by State Government, was conveyed by Gol on 9 June 2011. The
grant (® 9.36 crore) received from Gol was transferred (January 2012) by the
department to personal deposit account of Commissioner, TAD, Udaipur, for
implementation of the programme.

On a presentation made by Acetel Technologies (July 2011) before the
department, the Commissioner observed (August 2011) more shortcomings 6
in it and proposed to State Government for selection of agency through open
tenders to ensure transparency. The State Government also noticed (February
2012) that the procedure adopted for selecting the implementing agency
(Acetel) was not clear and transparent and therefore cancelled all its financial
sanctions and revoked orders of conducting the said training through Acetel.
The unspent grant was initially deposited in government treasury which was
finally adjusted by Gol against the subsequent grants to State Government
under SCA to TSP.

Thus, selection of agency without following proper procedure led to
cancellation and revoking of the training programme and non-utilisation of
special central assistance of € 9.36 crore by the State Government.

Admitting (March 2014) the facts of cancellation of the training programme
due to selection of Acetel without following a transparent procedure, the
department argued that there was no financial irregularity as the funds were
not misutilised. The reply was not acceptable as cancellation and revoking of
the training programme resulted in non-utilisation of हैं 9.36 crore and
deprivation of tribal students from job opportunities.

etc.

Slow pace of responses to audit findings.

According to rule 327(1) of General Financial and Accounts Rules, the
retention period for various accounting records ranged between one and three
years after audit. Non-compliance with the observations of Inspection Reports
(IRs) within the prescribed retention period is fraught with the possibility of
non settlement due to non-availability of records.

The position of IRs/paragraphs issued and settled during the period 2013-14
and pendency as on 31 March 2014, is shown in the following table:

The position reflects that 5,692 IRs containing 22,725 paragraphs issued
during the period 1992-93 to 2013-14 (up to September 2013) were pending
for settlement, indicating slow pace of responses against the IRs/paragraphs
from government departments.

Analysis of 759 IRs issued to various units of Medical Department (665 IRs)
and Labour Department (94 IRs) revealed that 2,945 paragraphs were
outstanding as on 31 March 2014. Category-wise details of irregularities
commented in IRs are given in the Appendix 3.3.

For early settlement of outstanding IRs and paragraphs, the State Government
issued (August 1969) instructions to all Departmental officers for sending the
first reply to IRs within a month and replies to further audit observations
within a fortnight. These instructions have been reiterated from time to time.
The instructions issued in March 2002 envisaged appointment of nodal
officers and Departmental Committee in each of the Administrative
Department to ensure compliance of all the matters relating to audit. Latest
instructions were issued in January 2010.

130


Chapter III Compliance Audit

The Finance Department issued (November 2004) instructions for conducting
four Audit Committee Meetings per year. Only 36 Audit Committee meetings
were held by 21 Departments during 2013-14.

Audit is an aid to management for good governance. The Government should
look into the matter and put in place control procedures to ensure that action is
taken against the defaulting officials and recoveries of losses/outstanding
advances/ overpayments are made in a time bound manner.

The matter was referred to State Government in September 2014; reply is
awaited (November 2014)
